Figure 16:
One-dimensional
\Delta\chi^{2}
profiles for the six oscillation parameters for normal (NO) and inverted (IO) ordering, in blue and purple, respectively.

Precision measurements of the oscillation parameters and the determination of the neutrino mass scale serve as a guide for theoretical particle physics. For instance, extensions of the Standard Model often aim to explain simultaneously the origin neutrino mass and the differences between the mixing in the leptonic and the quark sectors. Besides that, the question of CP violation in the neutrino sector can be related to the origin of the matter-antimatter asymmetry. Hence, an accurate measurement of these parameters can falsify model-building proposals.

Regarding neutrino phenomenology, in light of the precision being achieved in current experiments and the expectations for next-generation ones, it is possible to search for BSM physics in the neutrino sector. Deviations from the three-neutrino picture or tensions between experimental results could be interpreted as a signature of new physics.

Finally, on the experimental side, improving the existing results is pushing the development of detector technologies, like Liquid Argon time projector chambers (LAr TPCs), and novel analysis techniques, including those based on Machine Learning, among others
[
208
]
. Likewise, the measurement of Coherent Elastic Neutrino-Nucelus Scattering (CE
\nu
NS) has opened a new window for the study of neutrinos
[
209
]
. For instance, current and future detectors based on this process would be sensitive to the solar neutrino flux
[
210
,
211
]
. However, being a flavour-blind process at tree level, it is not that suitable for the study of oscillations.

In the years to come, substantial improvement is expected from next-generation experiments, including JUNO
[
212
]
, DUNE
[
213
]
, Hyper-Kamiokande
[
214
]
, KM3NET/ORCA
[
215
]
, the IceCube Upgrade
[
216
]
and ESS
\nu
SB
[
217
]
, among others. They have been conceived as multi-purpose experiments with the capability to reach unprecedented sensitivity and simultaneously explore neutrinos from different sources and at different energy ranges.

\pagelayout

wide
\addpart
Neutrino properties beyond masses and mixing

\pagelayout
margin

Chapter 4
Neutrino magnetic moments and spin-flavour precession in the Sun

Since neutrinos are electrically neutral, they do not couple directly to electromagnetic fields. Then, their electromagnetic properties originate through radiative corrections. For instance, in the Standard Model, neutrinos have a non-zero charge radius generated by quantum loop effects. Technically, that is the only non-zero electromagnetic form factor massless left-handed Weyl neutrino can have. Other non-zero neutrino electromagnetic properties are a common prediction in many extensions of the Standard Model accounting for neutrino masses. The focus of this chapter is the study of neutrino magnetic dipole moments through the process of spin-flavour precession (SFP). If neutrinos are Majorana particles, the interaction of their flavour off-diagonal magnetic moments with the solar magnetic field can result in the conversion of a fraction of left-handed
\nu_{e}
produced in the Sun into right-handed antineutrinos
\bar{\nu}_{\mu}
and
\bar{\nu}_{\tau}
. Although at present it is firmly established that the observed deficit of solar
\nu_{e}
is due to the MSW effect, SFP could still be present at a subdominant level.
The combined action of neutrino SFP and flavour conversions would then produce a small but potentially observable flux of solar electron antineutrinos
\bar{\nu}_{e}
at the Earth — see for instance
[
218
,
219
]
and references therein. The detection of such a flux would therefore be a clear signature of both non-zero magnetic moment and the Majorana nature of neutrinos.

In the first place,
Section
13
introduces neutrino electromagnetic properties in the one-photon approximation and, in particular, neutrino magnetic dipole moments. Then, we present an analytical calculation of the SFP for solar neutrinos in
Section
14
and check the validity of our results in
Section
15
. Finally, we conclude with some discussion of the results in
Section
16
.

Section 13
Neutrino magnetic moments

Figure 17:
Effective electromagnetic vertex accounting for neutrino electromagnetic interactions in the one-photon approximation.

In the one-photon approximation, electromagnetic interactions of the neutrino fields can be described by the effective electromagnetic vertex
[
105
]

\displaystyle\Lambda^{ij}_{\mu}(q)=(\gamma_{\mu}-q_{\mu}\not{q}/q^{2})\left[\mathsf{f}^{ij}_{Q}(q^{2})+\mathsf{f}^{ij}_{A}(q^{2})q^{2}\gamma_{5}\right]

\displaystyle-i\sigma_{\mu\nu}q^{\nu}[\mathsf{f}^{ij}_{M}(q^{2})+i\mathsf{f}^{ij}_{E}(q^{2})\gamma_{5}]\,,

(102)

as shown in
Figure

17
. It depends solely on the momentum transferred to the photon,
q
, and on the electromagnetic form factors
\mathsf{f}^{ij}_{x}
, with
x=Q,A,M,E
. The two superindices
i
and
j
account for the two neutrino mass states involved in the interaction. Electromagnetic form factors with
i=j
are often referred to as
diagonal
, whereas those with
i\neq j
are known as
transition
form factors. For an interaction with a real photon,
q^{2}=0
, we define the neutrino charge
\mathsf{q}_{ij}=\mathsf{f}^{ij}_{Q}(0)
, the magnetic and electric dipole moment — denoted by
\mathsf{\mu}=\mathsf{f}^{ij}_{M}(0)
and
\mathsf{\epsilon}=\mathsf{f}^{ij}_{E}(0)
respectively — and the anapole moment
\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{f}^{ij}_{A}(0)
.
7
7

7

Note that the matrices of electromagnetic form factors are Hermitian. In the case of Majorana neutrinos, charge, magnetic and electric form factor matrices are antisymmetric and the anapole form factor matrix is symmetric.

From this moment on, the discussion will focus on neutrino magnetic dipole moments. Neutrino electromagnetic dipole moment interactions flip chirality since

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{i}\sigma^{\mu\nu}\nu_{j}=\bar{\nu}_{iL}\sigma^{\mu\nu}\nu_{jR}+\bar{\nu}_{iR}\sigma^{\mu\nu}\nu_{jL}\,,

\displaystyle i\bar{\nu}_{i}\sigma^{\mu\nu}\gamma_{5}\nu_{j}=i\bar{\nu}_{iL}\sigma^{\mu\nu}\nu_{jR}-i\bar{\nu}_{iR}\sigma^{\mu\nu}\nu_{jL}\,.

(103)

Notice therefore that massless neutrinos have zero electromagnetic dipole moments.

Generically, neutrino magnetic moments are expected to be proportional to neutrino masses. For instance, in the minimal extension of the Standard Model with right-handed singlet fermions, neutrinos are Dirac fermions and their magnetic moments are
[
102
]

\displaystyle\mu^{D}_{ij}\simeq\frac{3eG_{\text{F}}}{16\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}}(m_{i}+m_{j})\left(\delta_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l=e,\,\mu,\,\tau}U^{*}_{lj}U_{li}\frac{m^{2}_{l}}{m^{2}_{W}}\right)\,.

(104)

In the expression above,
G_{\text{F}}
denotes the Fermi constant and
e
is the electron charge. The result depends on the mass of each neutrino,
m_{i}
, and the ratio between the charged-lepton masses,
m_{l}
, and the W-boson mass,
m_{W}
. This ratio is
5\times 10^{-4}
for the tau lepton, which means that transition magnetic moments are around three to four orders of magnitude smaller than the diagonal ones
[
105
]
,

\displaystyle\mu^{D}_{kk}\sim 10^{-20}\mu_{B}\left(\frac{m_{k}}{0.1\text{eV}}\right)\,,

(105)

where
\mu_{B}
denotes the Bohr magneton.

In the simplest extensions of the Standard Model leading to Majorana neutrinos — i.e. in the seesaw models presented in
Chapter
2
— and neglecting the model dependent diagrams arising from differences in the scalar potential, the magnetic moments read
[
102
]

\displaystyle\mu^{M}_{ij}\simeq-i\frac{3eG_{\text{F}}}{32\sqrt{2}\pi^{2}}(m_{i}+m_{j})\sum_{l=e,\,\mu,\,\tau}\text{Im}[U^{*}_{li}U_{lj}]\frac{m^{2}_{l}}{m^{2}_{W}}\,.

(106)

In this case, only transition magnetic moments are allowed, and the largest expected values are
\mathcal{O}(10^{-23}\mu_{B})

[
105
]
.

In several extensions of the Standard Model, neutrino magnetic moments are enhanced and reach values much closer to the existing experimental limits. For instance, in left-right symmetric models, the right-handed neutrino couples to a
W_{R}
gauge boson — which also mixes with the Standard Model
W
boson. In most minimal versions of these models, the magnetic moment for Dirac neutrinos becomes proportional to the charged-lepton masses, instead of being proportional to neutrino masses
[
102
,
220
,
221
]
. This leads to a significant enhancement of its value. However, it is also proportional to the mixing between the
W
and
W_{R}
bosons. Existing constraints on the parameter space of the minimal models imply that magnetic moments are at most
\mathcal{O}(10^{-16}\mu_{B})

[
105
]
.

A different family of models is the one featuring new light particles — with masses below
\sim
1 GeV — and a fractional charge — as large as possible given the existing constraints from millicharged particles. In these scenarios, the expected neutrino magnetic moments would be smaller than
\mathcal{O}(10^{-15}\mu_{B})

[
222
]
. Besides that, in the context of Supersymmetry, it is also possible to achieve relatively large neutrino magnetic moments
[
223
]
. As an example, values of the order
\mathcal{O}(10^{-14}\mu_{B})
can be reached in the Minimal Supersymmetric Standard Model extended with one vector-like lepton generation
[
224
]
.

Typically, theoretical models that predict large magnetic moments also predict large neutrino masses — incompatible with existing data — unless some fine tuning is invoked. A solution to this issue would be to suppress the mass-to-magnetic-moment ratio by some symmetry. Along these lines, one can propose a horizontal SU(2)
ν
symmetry that transforms
\nu
into
\nu^{C}
— the left-handed antiparticle of the right-handed neutrino
[
225
,
226
]
. If this symmetry was exact, the neutrino mass term would be forbidden but not the magnetic moment one. If this symmetry only holds approximately, then the ratio between masses and magnetic moments could still be small. In this way, magnetic moments of
\mathcal{O}(10^{-11}\mu_{B})
would be consistent with current limits on neutrino masses
[
227
,
226
]
. A different approach consists in suppressing the neutrino mass diagram without suppressing the magnetic moment one. As an example, this can be achieved with the inclusion of three Higgs doublets and a charged scalar that is a singlet of SU(2)
L

[
228
]
. In this case, spin conservation can be used to suppress neutrino masses with respect to magnetic moments. This spin symmetry mechanism allows reaching values of the magnetic moment of
\mathcal{O}(10^{-12}\mu_{B})

[
228
,
229
,
222
,
226
]
.

Figure 18:
Typical values of the neutrino magnetic moment predicted in different extensions of the Standard Model, as commented in the main text.

Figure

18
presents a graphical summary of the typical magnitude of the magnetic moments predicted in different theoretical models, as in
[
230
]
. In Appendix
11
, an updated compilation of limits on neutrino magnetic moments is presented, for comparison with the predictions here discussed. It also serves to contextualise the results derived in the following sections from spin-flavour precession.

Section 14
Spin-flavour precession in the Sun

From this section on, this chapter is restricted to the discussion of
spin-flavour
precession for Majorana neutrinos. The reason is that, in the case of Dirac neutrinos, the signature expected from SPF is a reduction of the overall flux of solar neutrinos due to the conversion of left-handed neutrino fields into the inert right-handed component. Given the current limits on neutrino magnetic moments and the uncertainties in the flux prediction, such an effect would be practically unobservable. However, for Majorana neutrinos, spin-flavour precession, together with flavour conversion, would result in a flux of antineutrinos detectable at neutrino observatories searching for a flux of
\bar{\nu}_{e}
of astrophysical origin.

The evolution of neutrinos in the Sun, in the absence of interactions with the magnetic field, is given by Equation
60
. However, in the current scenario, it is more convenient to define a new basis as follows

\displaystyle\nu_{flL}=O_{23}\Gamma_{\delta}O_{13}\nu^{\prime}_{L}\,,

(107)

where
\nu_{flL}=(\nu_{eL},\,\nu_{\mu L},\,\nu_{\tau L})^{T}
is the three-vector of amplitudes in flavour basis and
\nu^{\prime}_{L}=(\nu^{\prime}_{eL},\,\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L},\,\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L})^{T}
is the three-vector in the so-called
primed basis
. Here
O_{ij}
are the orthogonal matrices of rotation with the angle
\theta_{ij}
in the
i-j
plane and
\Gamma_{\delta}=\text{diag}(1,\,1,\,e^{i\delta_{\text{CP}}})
. With this notation, the lepton flavour mixing matrix is expressed as

\displaystyle U=O_{23}\Gamma_{\delta}O_{13}\Gamma^{\dagger}_{\delta}O_{12}\,.

(108)

In the case of Majorana neutrinos, the leptonic mixing matrix depends on two additional phases,
U_{M}=U\cdot K
, where
K=\text{diag}(1,e^{i\lambda_{2}},e^{i\lambda_{3}})
.

In this new basis, the evolution is given by

\displaystyle i\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}\begin{pmatrix}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}\\
\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}\\
\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}\end{pmatrix}=H\begin{pmatrix}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}\\
\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}\\
\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(109)

with

\displaystyle H=\begin{pmatrix}2\delta s^{2}_{12}+c^{2}_{13}V_{e}+V_{n}&2\delta s_{12}c_{12}&s_{13}c_{13}V_{e}\\
2\delta s_{12}c_{12}&2\delta c^{2}_{12}+V_{n}&0\\
c_{13}s_{13}V_{e}&0&2\Delta+s^{2}_{13}V_{e}+V_{n}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(110)

where we have introduced the short-hand notation
c_{ij}\equiv\cos\theta_{ij}
and
s_{ij}\equiv\sin\theta_{ij}
. Remember that the evolution for antineutrinos is computed directly taking into account that they are CP conjugate fields. This is equivalent to changing
V_{e}\rightarrow-V_{e}
,
V_{n}\rightarrow-V_{n}
and
\delta_{\text{CP}}\rightarrow-\delta_{\text{CP}}
. We will denote the Hamiltonian dictating antineutrino evolution by
\bar{H}
.

Once one includes the effect of neutrino interactions with external magnetic fields due to non-zero magnetic moments, the evolution equation as a function of the position
r
in the trajectory reads
[
231
,
232
,
233
]

i\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}r}\begin{pmatrix}\nu^{\prime}_{L}\\
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{R}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}H&\mathcal{B}\\
\mathcal{B}^{\dagger}&\bar{H}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\nu^{\prime}_{L}\\
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{R}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(111)

where
\bar{\nu}_{R}^{\prime}=(\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR}\;\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}\;\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R})^{T}
is the vector of the right-handed antineutrino amplitudes in the
primed basis
. The submatrices
H
and
\bar{H}
are defined in Equation
110
and in the subsequent description. The matrix
\mathcal{B}
, describing Majorana neutrino interactions with the external magnetic field, can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{B}_{e^{\prime}e^{\prime}}&\mathcal{B}_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}&\mathcal{B}_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\\
\mathcal{B}_{\mu^{\prime}e^{\prime}}&\mathcal{B}_{\mu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}&\mathcal{B}_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\\
\mathcal{B}_{\tau^{\prime}e^{\prime}}&\mathcal{B}_{\tau^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}&\mathcal{B}_{\tau^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}&\mu_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\\
-\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}&0&\mu_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\\
-\mu_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}&-\mu_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}&0\end{pmatrix}B_{\perp}(r)e^{i\phi(r)}\equiv\upmu^{\prime}\cdot B_{\perp}(r)e^{i\phi(r)}\,.

(112)

Here
\upmu^{\prime}
denotes the matrix of transition magnetic moments in the primed basis. For simplicity, we have introduced the short-hand notation
\mu_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
for the elements in the matrix
\upmu^{\prime}
. The factor
B_{\perp}(r)e^{i\phi(r)}
describes the external magnetic field in the plane transverse to the neutrino momentum, where
B_{\perp}(r)>0
is the field strength and the azimuthal angle
\phi(r)
defines the direction of the magnetic field in this plane. The contribution of the longitudinal component of the magnetic field is inversely proportional to the Lorenz factor of the neutrino and hence, it is negligible in all situations of practical interest.

The magnetic moments in the
primed basis
can be related to the magnetic moments in the mass basis,
\upmu_{m}
, which are of a more fundamental nature, namely

\upmu^{\prime}=\Gamma_{\delta}O_{12}K^{*}\upmu_{m}K^{*}O^{T}_{12}\Gamma_{\delta}\,.

(113)

Then, one finds the following relations

\displaystyle\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{12}e^{-i\lambda_{2}}\,,

(114)

\displaystyle\mu_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{13}c_{12}+\mu_{23}s_{12}e^{-i\lambda_{2}}\right)e^{-i(\lambda_{3}-\delta_{\text{CP}})}\,,

(115)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mu_{23}c_{12}e^{-i\lambda_{2}}-\mu_{13}s_{12}\right)e^{-i(\lambda_{3}-\delta_{\text{CP}})}\,,

(116)

which depend on the mixing angles and the CP-violating phases previously defined.

Taking into account that the diagonal elements of the matrix
\mathcal{B}
vanish — see Equation
112
— and
H_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}=H_{\tau^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}=\bar{H}_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}=\bar{H}_{\tau^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}
are also zero, the Equation
111
can be rewritten more explicitly, namely

\displaystyle i\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}r}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}=H_{e^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}+H_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}

\displaystyle+H_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{B}_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}+\mathcal{B}_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}\,,

(117a)

\displaystyle i\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}r}\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}=H_{\mu^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}+H_{\mu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{B}_{\mu^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR}+\mathcal{B}_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}\,,

(117b)

\displaystyle i\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}r}\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}=H_{\tau^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}+H_{\tau^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{\tau^{\prime}L}

\displaystyle+\mathcal{B}_{\tau^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR}+\mathcal{B}_{\tau^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}\,,

(117c)

\displaystyle i\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}r}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR}=\bar{H}_{e^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR}+\bar{H}_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}

\displaystyle+\bar{H}_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}

\displaystyle-\mathcal{B}^{*}_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}-\mathcal{B}^{*}_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}\,,

(117d)

\displaystyle i\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}r}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}=\bar{H}_{\mu^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR}+\bar{H}_{\mu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}

\displaystyle-\mathcal{B}^{*}_{\mu^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}-\mathcal{B}^{*}_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}\,,

(117e)

\displaystyle i\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}r}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}=\bar{H}_{\tau^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR}+\bar{H}_{\tau^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}

\displaystyle-\mathcal{B}^{*}_{\tau^{\prime}e^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}-\mathcal{B}^{*}_{\tau^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}\,.

(117f)

Subsection 14.1
Analytical solution to the evolution of the amplitudes

Since Majorana neutrinos can only have non-zero transition magnetic moments, direct conversion of
\nu_{eL}
produced in the Sun into
\bar{\nu}_{eR}
is not possible. Nonetheless, the transition can still occur in two steps, i.e.

\displaystyle\nu_{eL}\overset{\text{osc.}}{\longrightarrow}\nu_{\mu L}\overset{\text{SFP}}{\longrightarrow}\bar{\nu}_{eR}\,,

(118a)

\displaystyle\nu_{eL}\overset{\text{SFP}}{\longrightarrow}\bar{\nu}_{\mu R}\overset{\text{osc.}}{\longrightarrow}\bar{\nu}_{eR}\,,

(118b)

and similarly for transitions involving
\nu_{\tau L}
and
\bar{\nu}_{\tau R}
as intermediate states. In vacuum, the amplitudes of the processes in (
118a
) and (
118b
) are of opposite signs and cancel each other exactly at first order. In the Sun, due to matter effects, the cancellation is not exact but conversions remain strongly suppressed. The same argument applies to the transitions between states in the
primed basis
,
\nu_{eL}^{\prime}\to\bar{\nu}_{eR}^{\prime}
.
8
8

8

Note that in the presence of a non-zero twist in the magnetic field — i.e. if the azimuthal angle
\phi
in the parametrisation of the magnetic field changes
\text{d}\phi/\text{d}r\neq 0
— the cancellation is also not exact
[
233
]
.

Nonetheless, if flavour conversions and spin-flavour precession took place in spatially separated regions, the transitions
\nu_{eL}\to\bar{\nu}_{eR}
trough the processes in (
118a
) and (
118b
) — and the analogous ones with
\nu_{\tau L}
and
\bar{\nu}_{\tau R}
as intermediate states —- would not be suppressed.
Magnetic fields outside the Sun are very weak and thus, the only possibility left is that SPF occurs inside the Sun and subsequently, flavour conversions take place between the Sun and the Earth. Hence, in order to calculate the flux of solar electron antineutrinos, one first needs to know the flux of antineutrinos at the surface of the Sun.

To approach this problem analytically we proceed to make the following approximations in the set of Equations
117
. In the first place, and owing to the arguments presented above, one can neglect
\nu^{\prime}_{eL}\to\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR}
inside the Sun. Consequently, we skip the evolution equation for electron antineutrinos and we disregard the terms proportional to the
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR}
amplitude in the remaining equations. Secondly, we omit from the evolution equations the terms related to
H_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}=H_{\tau^{\prime}e^{\prime}}
and
\bar{H}_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}=\bar{H}_{\tau^{\prime}e^{\prime}}
since they are considerably smaller than the diagonal elements in the Hamiltonian
H_{\mu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}
,
H_{\tau^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}
,
\bar{H}_{\mu^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}
and
\bar{H}_{\tau^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}
. Physically, it can be interpreted as neglecting transitions between
\nu^{\prime}_{e}
and
\nu^{\prime}_{\tau}
, which are strongly suppressed by the smallness of
\theta_{13}

[
234
]
. Lastly, given the agreement between solar data and the three-neutrino oscillation picture, SFP can only be a subdominant effect. It is therefore justified to work at first order in perturbation theory in
\mathcal{B}
. To do so, we will obtain the evolution of the
\nu^{\prime}_{eL}
,
\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}
and
\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}
at zeroth order. Those amplitudes appear in the evolution equations of
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}
multiplied by elements of
\mathcal{B}
, which means that they act as sources for the appearance of both muon and tau antineutrinos.

With these considerations, the system of equations can be simplified to

\displaystyle i\frac{d}{dr}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}

\displaystyle=\left(2\delta s^{2}_{12}+c^{2}_{13}V_{e}+V_{n}\right)\nu^{\prime}_{eL}+2\delta s_{12}c_{12}\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}\,,

(119a)

\displaystyle i\frac{d}{dr}\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}

\displaystyle=2\delta s_{12}c_{12}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}+\left(2\delta c^{2}_{12}+V_{n}\right)\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}\,,

(119b)

\displaystyle i\frac{d}{dr}\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}

\displaystyle=\left(2\Delta+s^{2}_{13}V_{e}+V_{n}\right)\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}\,,

(119c)

\displaystyle i\frac{d}{dr}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}

\displaystyle=\left(2\delta c^{2}_{12}-V_{n}\right)\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mu^{*}_{e^{\prime}\bar{\mu}^{\prime}}B_{\perp}e^{-i\phi}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}-\mu^{*}_{\mu^{\prime}\bar{\tau}^{\prime}}B_{\perp}e^{-i\phi}\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}\,,

(119d)

\displaystyle i\frac{d}{dr}\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}

\displaystyle=\left(2\Delta-s^{2}_{13}V_{e}-V_{n}\right)\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mu^{*}_{e^{\prime}\bar{\tau}^{\prime}}B_{\perp}e^{-i\phi}\nu^{\prime}_{eL}+\mu^{*}_{\mu^{\prime}\bar{\tau}^{\prime}}B_{\perp}e^{-i\phi}\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}\,.

(119e)

Notice that with these approximations, the evolution of the amplitudes
\nu^{\prime}_{eL}
and
\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}
decouples from the rest of the system and reduces to solving the standard picture of solar neutrinos.

Let us denote the coordinate of the neutrino production point as
r_{0}
and define

E_{1,2}\equiv\delta+c^{2}_{13}\frac{V_{e}}{2}+V_{n}\mp\sqrt{\left(\delta\cos 2\theta_{12}-c^{2}_{13}V_{e}/2\right)^{2}+\delta^{2}\sin^{2}2\theta_{12}}\,,

(120)

and the effective mixing angle in matter,
\tilde{\theta}
, from the relation

\cos 2\tilde{\theta}(r)=\frac{\cos 2\theta_{12}-c^{2}_{13}V_{e}/2\delta}{\sqrt{\left(\cos 2\theta_{12}-c^{2}_{13}V_{e}/2\delta\right)^{2}+\sin^{2}2\theta_{12}}}\,.

(121)

Then, using the adiabatic approximation, one obtains

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}_{eL}(r)=c_{13}\left[\cos\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\cos\tilde{\theta}(r)e^{-i\int_{r_{0}}^{r}E_{1}dr^{\prime}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.+\sin\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\sin\tilde{\theta}(r)e^{-i\int_{r_{0}}^{r}E_{2}dr^{\prime}}\right]\,,

(122)

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}(r)=c_{13}\left[-\cos\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\sin\tilde{\theta}(r)e^{-i\int_{r_{0}}^{r}E_{1}dr^{\prime}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.+\sin\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\cos\tilde{\theta}(r)e^{-i\int_{r_{0}}^{r}E_{2}dr^{\prime}}\right]\,,

(123)

Here, we have also imposed the initial condition that neutrinos are produced as
\nu_{eL}
, which in the
primed basis
translates to
\nu^{\prime}_{eL}(r_{0})=c_{13}
,
\nu^{\prime}_{\mu L}(r_{0})=0
and
\nu^{\prime}_{\tau_{L}}(r_{0})=s_{13}
.

The evolution of the amplitude
\nu_{\tau L}^{\prime}
is decoupled from the rest of the system and given by

\nu^{\prime}_{\tau L}(r)=s_{13}e^{-i\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\left(2\Delta+s^{2}_{13}V_{e}+V_{n}\right)dr^{\prime}}.

(124)

Having found the evolution for
\nu_{eL}^{\prime}
,
\nu_{\mu L}^{\prime}
and
\nu_{\tau L}^{\prime}
amplitudes, one can solve Equation
119d
and Equation
119e
to find the amplitudes of
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}
. At the surface of the Sun — which we denote by
r=R_{\odot}
— they read

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}(R_{\odot})

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{0}}^{R_{\odot}}\text{d}r\,B_{\perp}(r)\left[c_{13}\mu^{*}_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\cos\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\cos\tilde{\theta}(r)e^{-ig_{1}(r)}\right.\,

\displaystyle\,+c_{13}\mu^{*}_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\sin\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\sin\tilde{\theta}(r)e^{-ig_{2}(r)}\left.-s_{13}\mu^{*}_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}e^{-ig_{3}(r)}\right]\,,

(125)

and

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}(R_{\odot})

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{0}}^{R_{\odot}}\text{d}r\,B_{\perp}(r)c_{13}\,\times

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt\left[\cos\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\left(\mu^{*}_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\cos\tilde{\theta}(r)-\mu^{*}_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\sin\tilde{\theta}(r)\right)e^{-ig_{4}(r)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 8.5359pt+\sin\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\left(\mu^{*}_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\sin\tilde{\theta}(r)+\mu^{*}_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\cos\tilde{\theta}(r)\right)e^{-ig_{5}(r)}\right]\,,

(126)

where we have defined the following functions,

\displaystyle g_{1,2}(r)\equiv\phi+\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\left[E_{1,2}-\left(2c^{2}_{12}\delta-V_{n}\right)\right]dr^{\prime}\,,

(127a)

\displaystyle g_{3}(r)\equiv\phi+\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\left[\left(2\Delta+s^{2}_{13}V_{e}+V_{n}\right)-\left(2c^{2}_{12}\delta-V_{n}\right)\right]dr^{\prime}\,,

(127b)

\displaystyle g_{4,5}(r)\equiv\phi+\int_{r_{0}}^{r}\left[E_{1,2}-\left(2\Delta-s^{2}_{13}V_{e}-V_{n}\right)\right]dr^{\prime}\,.

(127c)

Besides, we have omitted the irrelevant overall phase factors from the expressions for
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau}
since, when propagating to Earth, coherence is lost and such phase factors become inconsequential. From this point on, we consistently disregard all phase factors of this sort.

In the remaining part of this section, we discuss the details of how to obtain an analytical expression for the amplitude with an extensive discussion on the role of the magnetic field twist — i.e. if the azimuthal angle
\phi
in the parametrisation of the magnetic field varies along the neutrino trajectory.

Analytical expressions for the amplitudes

The integrals in the amplitudes
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau}
, in the set of Equations
125
and
126
, are of the form

I=\int_{a}^{b}f(x)e^{-ig(x)}dx\,,

(128)

where
f(x)
is a slowly varying function of coordinate and
|g^{\prime}(x)|
is large except possibly in the vicinity of a finite number of points in the interval
(a,b)
. Such integrals get their main contributions from the endpoints of the integration intervals and the static points of
g(x)
— i.e. where
g^{\prime}(x)=0
— if any
[
235
]
. This is known as the
stationary phase approximation
. We will first check whether any stationary phase point exists for the integrals in Equations
125
and
126
.

Let us start with the amplitude
\bar{\nu}_{\mu R}^{\prime}
, from Equation
125
, which depends on the functions
g_{1}
,
g_{2}
and
g_{3}
. The stationary phase conditions are

\frac{d}{dr}g_{1,2}=0\indent\longrightarrow\indent\frac{d\phi}{dr}=2c^{2}_{12}\delta-V_{n}-E_{1,2}\,,\qquad\quad\penalty\ \penalty\

(129)

\frac{d}{dr}g_{3}=0\indent\longrightarrow\indent\frac{d\phi}{dr}=2c^{2}_{12}\delta-2V_{n}-2\Delta-s^{2}_{13}V_{e}\,.

(130)

Using the definition of
E_{1,2}
in Equation
120
, one can reduce Equation
129
to

\frac{\text{d}\phi}{\text{d}r}+2V_{n}+c^{2}_{13}V_{e}-2\delta\cos 2\theta_{12}=\frac{\delta^{2}\sin^{2}2\theta_{12}}{d\phi/dr+2V_{n}}\,,

(131)

which has a solution as long as

1+\sin^{2}2\theta_{12}\frac{c^{2}_{13}V_{e}}{d\phi/dr+2V_{n}}\geq 0\,.

(132)

This condition is satisfied if

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi}{dr}>2|V_{n}|\quad\text{or}\quad-\frac{d\phi}{dr}\geq c^{2}_{13}V_{e}\left(\sin^{2}2\theta_{12}-\frac{1-Y_{e}}{c^{2}_{13}Y_{e}}\right),

(133)

where
Y_{e}
is the number of electrons per nucleon in the medium. As
Y_{e}
varies between 0.67 and 0.88 in the Sun
[
236
]
, one can see that the expression in the brackets in the second condition in Equation
133
is positive and of the order of 0.3 – 0.7. Therefore, for non-twisting magnetic fields, the stationary phase condition cannot be fulfilled. In fact, it requires
|d\phi/dr|
to be of the same order of magnitude as
V_{e}
and
|V_{n}|
, which vary from
\sim 7\times 10^{-12}\text{ eV}
near the neutrino production point to zero at the surface of the Sun, where the solar magnetic field nearly vanishes as well.
One can see that the stationary phase condition can be fulfilled, for instance, for magnetic fields with a constant twist
|d\phi/dr|\sim 10/R_{\odot}\sim 3\times 10^{-15}\text{ eV}

[
233
]
.

Regarding Equation
130
, the term
2\Delta
on its right-hand side is at least an order of magnitude larger than the other terms.
9
9

9

Note that
2\Delta\sim 10^{-9}-10^{-10}
eV.
For the solution of this equation to exist,
d\phi/dr
should be of the same order of magnitude, which corresponds to
\sim 1-10
rad/km. While short-scale stochastic magnetic fields in the Sun may have such rapid twists, it is unlikely that this is possible for large-scale fields relevant for SFP.

Similarly, for the integrals in Equation
126
, corresponding to the amplitude
\bar{\nu}_{\tau R}^{\prime}
, the stationary phase points correspond to
\frac{d}{dr}g_{1,2,3}=0
, or

\frac{d}{dr}g_{4,5}=0\indent\longrightarrow\indent\frac{d\phi}{dr}=2\Delta-s^{2}_{13}V_{e}-V_{n}-E_{1,2}=0\,.

(134)

Again, the term
2\Delta
on the right side is larger than the other terms and, as the case of Equation
130
, the stationary phase condition is unlikely to be satisfied.

This means that no stationary phase points are expected for the integrals in Equation
126
and the integral containing
g_{3}
in Equation
125
, and they should receive their main contributions from the endpoints of the integration interval.

In the following, we will first focus on the case in which the magnetic fields in the Sun are either non-twisting or twist slowly enough so that no stationary phase points exist.
The effects of the possible existence of stationary phase points in the scenario with fast-twisting magnetic fields will be discussed later in the text.

Non-twisting or slowly twisting magnetic fields

In the case of a non-twisting magnetic field, the integrals we are interested in get contributions only from the endpoints of the integration interval.

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}

\displaystyle f(x)e^{-ig(x)}dx=\int_{a}^{b}\frac{f(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)}g^{\prime}(x)e^{-ig(x)}

\displaystyle=\left[i\frac{f(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)}e^{-ig(x)}\right]_{a}^{b}-i\int_{a}^{b}\left(\frac{f^{\prime}(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)}-\frac{f(x)g^{\prime\prime}(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)^{2}}\right)e^{-ig(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\left[\left(i\frac{f(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)}+\frac{f^{\prime}(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)^{2}}-\frac{f(x)g^{\prime\prime}(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)^{3}}\right)e^{-ig(x)}\right]_{a}^{b}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{g^{\prime}(x)^{3}}\right).

(135)

It follows from the definitions of the phases
g_{i}
— with
i=1,...,5
— in Equations
127
that in the case of interest to us the condition

|g^{\prime\prime}(x)|^{2}/g^{\prime}(x)^{2}\ll 1

(136)

is satisfied for all
g_{i}(x)
, mainly because the matter potentials are slowly varying functions of the position. Then, the third term in the brackets in Equation
135
is negligible when compared to the first term.

In addition, provided that

\Bigg|\frac{f(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}\Bigg|\gg\frac{1}{|g^{\prime}(x)|},

(137)

the first term dominates over the second one. The left-hand side of this inequality is essentially the characteristic distance over which
f
varies significantly — often referred to as the scale height of
f(x)
.
For the case under study,
f(x)
is proportional to
B_{\perp}(r)
times
\sin\tilde{\theta}(r)
or
\cos\tilde{\theta}(r)
. The effective mixing angle
\tilde{\theta}(r)
varies slowly inside the Sun and as a consequence, it is justified to assume adiabatic evolution for flavour conversions in the Sun. Therefore, the scale height of
f(x)
is basically that of the solar magnetic field, and Equation
137
reads

L_{B}\equiv\frac{B_{\perp}(r)}{|B^{\prime}_{\perp}(r)|}\gg\frac{1}{|g_{i}^{\prime}(r)|}.

(138)

for each of the five functions
g_{i}(r)
defined.

For neutrinos propagating in the Sun, the conditions in Equation
138
are satisfied if
L_{B}\gg 10^{-4}R_{\odot}
.
Solar magnetic fields with
L_{B}\lesssim 10^{-4}R_{\odot}
are not expected to exist over large distances in the Sun and hence, the condition
137
is satisfied. A short-scale magnetic field with a larger scale height could be present but it would not lead to sizeable SFP effects.
As a result, it is justified to keep only the first term in the brackets in Equation
135
. Moreover, since the magnetic field strength at the surface is negligibly weak, we only take into account the contribution from the production point
r=r_{0}
. Under these assumptions, and given that
g^{\prime}_{3}\sim 2\Delta
,
g^{\prime}_{4,5}\sim-2\Delta
and that

\bigg|\frac{1}{g^{\prime}_{4}}-\frac{1}{g^{\prime}_{5}}\bigg|\ll\bigg|\frac{1}{g^{\prime}_{4,5}}\bigg|,

(139)

the amplitudes
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}
from Equations
125
and
126
yield

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}(R_{\odot})

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt\simeq\,B_{\perp}(r_{0})\left[c_{13}\mu^{*}_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\left(\frac{\cos^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})}{g^{\prime}_{1}(r_{0})}+\frac{\sin^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})}{g^{\prime}_{2}(r_{0})}\right)-\frac{s_{13}\mu^{*}_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}}{2\Delta}\right],

(140)

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}(R_{\odot})\simeq\,B_{\perp}(r_{0})\frac{c_{13}\mu^{*}_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}}{2\Delta}\,.

(141)

Notice that setting
\theta_{13}=0
and neglecting
\cos\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})
compared with
\sin\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})
one recovers the expression for the amplitude of
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}
found in
[
218
]
.

Fast-twisting magnetic fields

For large twisting magnetic fields, if one of the conditions in
133
is satisfied, then the contribution of stationary phase points to the integrals in the amplitudes needs to be taken into account.

For a stationary phase point
x_{0}
, such that
g^{\prime}(x_{0})=0
, and being
f(x)
a slowly varying function of the coordinate,

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}f(x)e^{ig(x)}dx\simeq f(x_{0})e^{ig(x_{0})}\int_{a}^{b}\text{exp}\left[i\frac{g^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})}{2}(x-x_{0})^{2}\right]dx

\displaystyle=f(x_{0})e^{ig(x_{0})}\int_{a}^{b}\left[\cos\left(\frac{g^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})}{2}(x-x_{0})^{2}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\left.+i\sin\left(\frac{g^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})}{2}(x-x_{0})^{2}\right)\right]dx\,,

(142)

where we have expanded

g(x)\simeq g(x_{0})+\frac{1}{2}g^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})(x-x_{0})^{2}\,.

(143)

With an appropriate change of variable,
\pi t^{2}=g^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})(x-x_{0})^{2}
, and from the fact that

\int_{0}^{t_{a}}\cos\left(\frac{\pi t^{2}}{2}\right)\text{d}t=\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\int_{0}^{t_{a}}\sin\left(\frac{\pi t^{2}}{2}\right)\text{d}t=\frac{1}{2}\,,

(144)

when
t_{a}\,\longrightarrow\,\infty
, one obtains the following approximate solution for the integral

I\simeq f(x_{0})\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{g^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})}}e^{i\left(\frac{\pi}{4}+g(x_{0})\right)}\,.

(145)

Let
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
be such that
g^{\prime}_{1}(r_{1})=0
and
g^{\prime}_{2}(r_{2})=0
. Then, the contribution of the stationary phase points to the amplitude
\bar{\nu}_{\mu R}^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}(R_{\odot})=c_{13}\mu^{*}_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}\left[\cos\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\cos\tilde{\theta}(r_{1})B_{\perp}(r_{1})\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{g^{\prime\prime}_{1}(r_{1})}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt\left.+\sin\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\sin\tilde{\theta}(r_{2})B_{\perp}(r_{2})\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{g^{\prime\prime}_{2}(r_{2})}}e^{-i(g_{2}(r_{2})-g_{1}(r_{1}))}\right]\,,

(146)

This contribution dominates over the ones from the endpoints of the integration interval, which can therefore be neglected. Note that this is not the case for the amplitude
\bar{\nu}_{\tau R}^{\prime}
, which has no stationary phase points and consequently, it is given by Equation
141
, regardless of the twist of the magnetic field. Nonetheless, the validity of this approximation still relies on the assumption that there is no direct
\nu_{e}\rightarrow\bar{\nu}_{e}
in the Sun. In the presence of a large twisting magnetic field, this might not be accurate enough.

From this point on, we will limit ourselves to the study of a non-twisting or slowly twisting magnetic field. As follows from the discussion above, the contributions from stationary phase points are larger than the ones from the endpoints. Therefore, by focusing our work on the case of non-twisting magnetic fields, we will be deriving conservative upper bounds on
\mu B_{\perp}
.

Subsection 14.2
Solar electron antineutrino flux on Earth

Once we know the amplitudes
\bar{\nu}_{\mu R}^{\prime}
and
\bar{\nu}_{\tau R}^{\prime}
at the surface of the Sun, it is straightforward to compute the expected flux of electron antineutrinos that reaches the Earth.
Since the magnetic field between the Sun and the Earth is very weak, its effects are negligible and the evolution of neutrinos on their way to Earth is simply due to flavour transformations. Moreover, due to the loss of coherence, what arrives on Earth is an incoherent sum of mass eigenstates
[
237
]
, both for neutrinos and antineutrinos.
Thus, the electron antineutrino appearance probability on Earth is

P(\nu_{eL}\rightarrow\bar{\nu}_{eR})=|U_{e1}|^{2}|\bar{\nu}_{1\oplus}|^{2}+|U_{e2}|^{2}|\bar{\nu}_{2\oplus}|^{2}+|U_{e3}|^{2}|\bar{\nu}_{3\oplus}|^{2}\,,

(147)

where
\bar{\nu}_{i\oplus}
(
i=1,2,3
) denote the amplitudes of the antineutrino mass eigenstates reaching the Earth.

These amplitudes are related to those in the
primer basis
by
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{R}=\tilde{U}\bar{\nu}_{R}
, with
\tilde{U}=\Gamma^{\dagger}_{\delta}O_{12}
, and where
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{R}=(\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR},\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R},\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R})^{T}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{R}=(\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{eR},\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R},\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R})^{T}
. Therefore

|\bar{\nu}_{i\oplus}|^{2}=|\tilde{U}_{\mu^{\prime}i}|^{2}|\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu R}(R_{\odot})|^{2}+|\tilde{U}_{\tau^{\prime}i}|^{2}|\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\tau R}(R_{\odot})|^{2}\,,

(148)

and the electron antineutrino appearance probability can be rewritten as

\displaystyle P(\nu_{eL}\rightarrow\bar{\nu}_{eR})=\frac{1}{2}c^{2}_{13}\sin^{2}2\theta_{12}|\bar{\nu}_{\mu R}^{\prime}(R_{\odot})|^{2}+s^{2}_{13}|\bar{\nu}_{\tau R}^{\prime}(R_{\odot})|^{2}.

(149)

Substituting here the approximate analytical expressions for the amplitudes
\bar{\nu}_{\mu R}^{\prime}
and
\bar{\nu}_{\tau R}^{\prime}
from Equations
140
and
141
, we find

\displaystyle P(\nu_{eL}\rightarrow\bar{\nu}_{eR})=s^{2}_{13}B^{2}_{\perp}(r_{0})\Bigg(\frac{c_{13}|\mu_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}|}{2\Delta}\Bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt+\frac{1}{2}c^{2}_{13}\sin^{2}2\theta_{12}B^{2}_{\perp}(r_{0})\left[c^{2}_{13}|\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}|^{2}\Bigg(\frac{\cos^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})}{g^{\prime}_{1}(r_{0})}+\frac{\sin^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})}{g^{\prime}_{2}(r_{0})}\Bigg)^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 34.14322pt\left.-2\text{ Re}\{\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}^{*}\mu_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}\}\left(\frac{\cos^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})}{g^{\prime}_{1}(r_{0})}+\frac{\sin^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})}{g^{\prime}_{2}(r_{0})}\right)\frac{s_{13}c_{13}}{2\Delta}\right]\,.

(150)

The terms containing
|\mu_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}|^{2}
and
|\mu_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}|^{2}
are expected to give very small contributions since they are proportional to
s^{2}_{13}/\Delta^{2}
. This is true unless
|\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}|
were anomalously small with respect to the other transition magnetic moments.

There are three main differences between this result and that derived in the 2-flavour approach in
[
218
]
.
Firstly, the main term in Equation
150
, which is proportional to
|\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}|^{2}
contains an additional factor
c^{4}_{13}
that was not accounted for in the 2-neutrino result. Note that, interestingly, since
|\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}|^{2}
is equal to
|\mu_{12}|^{2}
, to a very good approximation and if all magnetic moments are of the same order of magnitude, the electron antineutrino appearance probability is proportional to
|\mu_{12}B_{\perp}(r_{0})|^{2}
.
The second difference is the cross-term contribution in Equation
150
involving
\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}
and
\mu_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}
, which is absent in the two-flavour approach. This additional term may result in a
non-negligible
correction to the
\bar{\nu}_{eR}
appearance probability.
Finally, the simplifying assumptions invoked in
[
218
]
restricted its applicability to neutrino energies above
\sim 5-8
MeV, whereas such limitation to the validity of the result is not present for Equation
150
. These and other aspects are discussed in detail in
Section
15
.

Subsection 14.3
Numerical calculations

As an alternative to the approximate analytical solution derived above, one could solve the complete set of six coupled differential equations — see Equations
117
— numerically, tracing the evolution of the amplitudes from the production point to the Earth.
We have developed a numerical code that performs this task and computes the electron antineutrino appearance probability at the surface of the Earth. The calculation averages over the production region of neutrinos in the Sun and also takes into account the profiles of electron and neutron number densities through which neutrinos propagate.

We are mainly interested in inverse beta decay as the detection channel since the strongest limits have been derived from this process, which has a threshold of
\sim
1.8 MeV. Thus, we will consider only the flux from
8
B.

We considered two Standard Solar Models (SMM) with different abundances of heavy elements and therefore having different metallicities. We present the results for the high-metallicity GS98 model and the low-metallicity AGSS09 model, as from
[
236
]
. Regarding the magnetic field inside the Sun, it is essentially unknown and one has to resort to model field profiles. Nevertheless, the arbitrariness of the modelling of this profile is partially alleviated because one expects the
\bar{\nu}_{e}
appearance probability to be most sensitive to the magnetic field strength at the neutrino production point. In order to prove this statement, we implement different magnetic field profiles with approximately the same strength at
r=0.05R_{\odot}
— see section
15.3
below.
If not otherwise specified, in our calculations, we assume the following linearly decreasing magnetic field profile,

B_{\perp}(r)=B_{0}(r)\equiv 52600(1-r/R_{\odot})\,{\text{kG}}\,,

(151)

which takes the value

B_{\perp}(r=r_{0})\simeq 5\times 10^{7}
G
at
r_{0}
= 0.05R
⊙
and vanishes at the surface of the Sun. The magnetic field inside the Sun is also assumed to be non-twisting.

Section 15
Results

Subsection 15.1
Comparison between analytical and numerical results

Since the main contribution to the electron antineutrino appearance probability is proportional to
|\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}|^{2}=|\mu_{12}|^{2}
, we will first set
\mu_{13}=0
and
\mu_{23}=0
— which also implies that
\mu_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}
and
\mu_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}
vanish. In this way, we will compare our analytical results with the ones from the numerical calculations and the previous analytical expression derived in the 2-neutrino framework. Under these assumptions, our analytical expression in Equation
150
becomes

\displaystyle P(\nu_{eL}\rightarrow\bar{\nu}_{eR})=

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\frac{1}{2}c^{4}_{13}\sin^{2}2\theta_{12}B^{2}_{\perp}(r_{0})|\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}|^{2}\Bigg(\frac{\cos^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})}{g^{\prime}_{1}(r_{0})}+\frac{\sin^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})}{g^{\prime}_{2}(r_{0})}\Bigg)^{2}.

(152)

We will also consider the simplified analytical expression

\displaystyle P(\nu_{eL}\rightarrow\bar{\nu}_{eR})_{\text{simpl.}}=\frac{1}{2}c^{4}_{13}\sin^{2}2\theta_{12}B^{2}_{\perp}(r_{0})|\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}|^{2}\Bigg(\frac{\sin^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})}{g^{\prime}_{2}(r_{0})}\Bigg)^{2},

(153)

obtained from Equation
152
by neglecting the first term in the brackets compared to the second one.
This approximation is similar to the one adopted in
[
218
]
and it
is expected to be valid for relatively high neutrino energies, for which
\cos^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\ll\sin^{2}\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})
.
10
10

10

Note that
|g_{1}^{\prime}(r_{0})|
and
|g_{2}^{\prime}(r_{0})|
differ by less than a factor of two for all considered energies.

Figure 19:
Comparison of different calculations for
\bar{\nu}_{eR}
appearance probability for
8
B neutrinos. We take the values
(
\sin^{2}\theta_{12}
,
\Delta m^{2}_{21}
) = (0.32, 7.5
\times 10^{-5}
eV
2
)
and
\sin^{2}\theta_{13}=0.022
for the oscillation parameters
[
126
]
. The left and right panels correspond to AGSS09 and GS98 SSM, respectively
[
236
]
.
The green curves show numerical calculation with averaging over the neutrino production region and the orange ones are the results based on the full analytical expression in Equation
152
. Grey dashed curves correspond to the simplified analytical expression in Equation
153
.

In
Figure

19
we compare our analytical results with those found by numerical solution of the Equations
117
. The green curves show the numerical results obtained, after averaging over the neutrino production region.
The solid orange curves and grey dashed ones correspond to the analytical expressions in Equation
152
and Equation
153
, respectively, assuming that all neutrinos are produced in the Sun at
r_{0}=0.05R_{\odot}
.
The left and right panels show the results for AGSS09 and GS98 solar models, respectively. The figure demonstrates a good general agreement between our numerical and analytical results, especially for neutrino energies
E\gtrsim 5
MeV. The discrepancy between the numerical and analytical results becomes larger for smaller
E
, where the
\bar{\nu}_{eR}
appearance probability is relatively small.

As shown in
Figure

19
, the
\bar{\nu}_{eR}
appearance probability in Equation
150
is a relatively slowly varying function of neutrino energy for
E\gtrsim 5-8
MeV, which is the relevant energy range for the experiments detecting mainly solar
8
B neutrinos. Taking for an estimate its value at
E=12
MeV and electron and neutron number densities at the neutrino production point
N_{e}\simeq 89
cm
-3
and
N_{n}\simeq 35
cm
-3

[
236
]
, the electron antineutrino appearance probability can be written as

P(\nu_{eL}\rightarrow\bar{\nu}_{eR})\simeq 1.1\times 10^{-10}\left(\frac{\mu_{12}B_{\perp}(r_{0})}{10^{-12}\mu_{B}\cdot 10\,\text{kG}}\right)^{2}\,,

(154)

where
\mu_{B}
is the electron Bohr magneton.
An expression of this form was derived in the two-flavour approach
[
218
]
and has been extensively used in the literature to derive limits on magnetic moments. That previous expression differs by a factor of 1.4 with respect to the one here presented. This is partly due to 3-flavour effects and the use of updated neutrino mixing parameters and solar models and partly because of the approximation
\cos\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})\ll\sin\tilde{\theta}(r_{0})
adopted in
[
218
]
.

Note that Equation
154
is not suitable for experiments sensitive to
pp
,
pep
or
7
Be solar neutrinos, for which the electron antineutrino appearance probability is strongly suppressed. See, for instance, that at
E\sim 1
MeV, the exact numerical result is approximately three orders of magnitude smaller than that given by Equation
154
and also exhibits a stronger energy dependence.

Subsection 15.2
Neutrino evolution inside the Sun

It is possible to gain a better understanding of the process of antineutrino appearance by considering the evolution of the system inside the Sun as a function of distance to the centre of the Sun. We do so in a two-flavour framework — setting
\sin^{2}\theta_{13}=0
. Since corrections are of order
\sin^{2}\theta_{13}
, the results derived in this way do not deviate significantly from those in the full 3-flavour approach.

In
Figure

20
we show the evolution of the antineutrino appearance probabilities as a function of the distance to the centre of the Sun. Results were obtained by numerically solving the evolution equations for mass-eigenstates — in the left panel — and
primed
states — in the right panel.

Figure 20:

Antineutrino appearance probabilities in the two-neutrino approach for the mass eigenstates and for the states in the
primed basis
— in the left and right panels respectively — as functions of the distance to the centre of the Sun. The neutrino energy is fixed to
E=10
MeV. Transition magnetic moments are
\mu_{12}=10^{-12}\mu_{B}
,
\mu_{13}=\mu_{23}=0
and the production point of electron neutrinos is
r_{0}=0.05R_{\odot}
.

From the right panel, one can see that considering that
\bar{\nu}_{e}^{\prime}\sim 0
is a reasonably good approximation inside the Sun. However, notice that it becomes less accurate when approaching the surface.

In this two-flavour approach, spin-flavour precession converts solar
\nu_{e}
into
\bar{\nu}_{\mu}^{\prime}
. Close to the production point, the composition of
\bar{\nu}_{\mu}^{\prime}
is approximately

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{\mu}^{\prime}\simeq-\sin\bar{\vartheta}(r_{0})\bar{\nu}_{1M}+\cos\bar{\vartheta}(r_{0})\bar{\nu}_{2M}\,.

(155)

Here,
\bar{\nu}_{1,2M}
are the states that diagonalise the antineutrino Hamiltonian in matter and the effective mixing angle
\bar{\vartheta}(r)
is

\displaystyle\tan 2\bar{\vartheta}(r)=\frac{\sin 2\theta_{12}}{\cos 2\theta_{12}+c^{2}_{13}V_{e}(r)/2\delta}\,.

(156)

Given the large electron number density around the neutrino production point, for neutrino energies
E\gtrsim 2\text{ MeV}
, the mixing is
\bar{\vartheta}(r_{0})\ll 1
and, therefore,
\bar{\nu}^{\prime}_{\mu}\simeq\bar{\nu}_{2M}
. Since antineutrinos evolve adiabatically in the Sun and there is no level crossing,
\bar{\nu}_{2M}
states propagate through the Sun without a noticeable transformation into
\bar{\nu}_{1M}
.
At the surface, matter density essentially vanishes. Hence, matter eigenstates become mass eigenstates there and antineutrinos emerge at the surface of the Sun as
\bar{\nu}_{2}
. This can be seen in
Figure

21
, where we depict the appearance probabilities for
\bar{\nu}_{1}
and
\bar{\nu}_{2}
— in the left panel — and for the matter eigenstates
\bar{\nu}_{1M}
and
\bar{\nu}_{2M}
— in the right panel. Notice that we have normalised the results to unit sum.

At a distance
r=0.1R_{\odot}
— which is relatively close to the neutrino production point — most of the antineutrinos are in the form of
\bar{\nu}_{2M}
states, which are a combination of
\bar{\nu}_{1}
and
\bar{\nu}_{2}
.
At the surface of the Sun, the antineutrinos emerge as
\bar{\nu}_{2M}
as well, which coincides there with
\bar{\nu}_{2}
. Again this is in agreement with the numerical results shown in the left panel of
Figure

20
, where one can see that at
r=R_{\odot}
we mainly find
\bar{\nu}_{2}
.
Since
\bar{\nu}_{2}
is a linear combination of
\bar{\nu}_{e}^{\prime}
and
\bar{\nu}_{\mu}^{\prime}
— with weights
\sin^{2}\theta_{12}\simeq 1/3
and
\cos^{2}\theta_{12}\simeq 2/3
respectively — at the surface of the Sun the appearance probability of
\bar{\nu}_{\mu}^{\prime}
is about twice that of
\bar{\nu}_{e}^{\prime}
— as shown in the right panel of
Figure

20
.
It should be noted that, unlike for the normalised probabilities shown in
Figure

21
, the sum of the antineutrino appearance probabilities presented in
Figure

20
is not conserved. This results from the fact that some of the antineutrinos can precess back to neutrinos while they evolve inside the Sun.

Figure 21:
Two-flavour evolution of antineutrino appearance probabilities inside the Sun for mass eigenstates and the matter eigenstates — in the left and right panel respectively — normalised to the unit total antineutrino appearance probability. The numerical values chosen for the neutrino energy, magnetic moment and the
\nu_{e}
production coordinate are the same as in
Figure

20
.

Subsection 15.3
The roles of various transition magnetic moments and the magnetic field profile

In the above, our numerical analysis was assuming only one transition magnetic moment — namely
\mu_{e^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}}=\mu_{12}
— to be nonzero. This was motivated by our analytical results, which showed that the contributions from
\mu_{e^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}
and
\mu_{\mu^{\prime}\tau^{\prime}}
— which are linear combinations
\mu_{13}
and
\mu_{23}
— are strongly suppressed.
This point is illustrated in
Figure

22
, where we present the
\bar{\nu}_{e}
appearance probability
P(\nu_{e}\to\bar{\nu}_{e})
at the Earth when one non-zero magnetic moment is allowed at a time. It demonstrates that unless
\mu_{13}
or
\mu_{23}
are more than one order of magnitude larger than
\mu_{12}
, the latter completely dominates the
\nu_{e}\to\bar{\nu}_{e}
conversion.

Figure 22:
Electron antineutrino appearance probability on the Earth as a function of neutrino energy for non-zero
\mu_{12}
,
\mu_{13}
and
\mu_{23}
. Results are shown for the AGSS09 solar model and the evolution equations were solved numerically.

Up until this point, all the numerical results were obtained assuming that the magnetic field strength decreases linearly as in Equation
151
. We shall now study how the solar magnetic field profile affects
\nu_{e}\to\bar{\nu}_{e}
conversion.
For this purpose, we compare the
\bar{\nu}_{e}
appearance probability for the linear profile we used above with those for two different parabolic profiles — which we denote by
B_{1}(r)
and
B_{2}(r)
. The three profiles are such that the strength at
R=0.5R_{\odot}
is
\sim 5\times 10^{4}
kG at
r=0.05R_{\odot}
and vanishes at the surface of the Sun.
The first profile,

B_{1}(r)=50000+2632\frac{r}{R_{\odot}}-52632\left(\frac{r}{R_{\odot}}\right)^{2}\,\text{kG}\,,

(157)

is almost flat over the production region whereas the second one,

B_{2}(r)=55000-102368\frac{r}{R_{\odot}}+47368\left(\frac{r}{R_{\odot}}\right)^{2}\,\text{kG}\,,

(158)

corresponds to the magnetic field that is smaller than the linear one for
r>0.05R_{\odot}
. A more detailed discussion on the possible magnitude of the magnetic field is carried out in
Section
16
.

In the left panel of
Figure

23
we present the magnetic field profiles we employ, whereas, in the right panel, we show the corresponding
\bar{\nu}_{e}
appearance probabilities. For neutrino energies
E\lesssim 7
MeV all the magnetic field profiles we considered lead to
\bar{\nu}_{e}
appearance probabilities that are quite similar to each other. Nevertheless, the differences between them and, therefore, the sensitivity to the magnetic field profile, increases with neutrino energy.
The reason for this is twofold. Firstly, neutrinos are produced over extended regions. Thus, the
\nu_{e}\to\bar{\nu}_{e}
production probability is sensitive to the magnetic profile in that region.
Secondly, the
\bar{\nu}_{e}
appearance probability depends on the effective mixing of the left-handed and right-handed neutrinos at their production point
r_{0}
, which is proportional to
\mu_{12}B_{\perp}(r_{0})/(\Delta m_{21}^{2}/2E)
and increases with the energy.
From
Figure

23
, it follows that for energies of
E\sim 8
MeV, the dependence of the electron antineutrino appearance probability on the choice of the magnetic field profile is approximately 10
-
15%.

Figure 23:
Benchmark magnetic field profiles inside the Sun,
as defined in Equation
151
, Equation
157
and Equation
158
and the corresponding electron antineutrino appearance probabilities on the Earth — in the left and right panel respectively. The results of the analytical expression are shown by a black dotted curve. For the rest of the curves evolution equations were solved numerically.

Subsection 15.4
Average
\mathbf{\bar{\nu}_{e}}
appearance probability and expected flux

Several experimental collaborations have reported upper bounds on electron antineutrino fluxes from astrophysical sources. These are obtained for different energy ranges as

\Phi_{\text{C.L.}}=\frac{N_{\text{C.L.}}}{\epsilon\cdot\langle\sigma\rangle\cdot T\cdot N_{p}}\,,

(159)

where
N_{\text{C.L.}}
denotes the upper limit on the number of events at a given confidence level,
\epsilon
is the average detection efficiency in the energy range considered,
\langle\sigma\rangle
is the averaged cross-section in the same energy range,
T
is the exposure time and
N_{p}
is the number of targets in the detector.

To facilitate the use of our results in ongoing and future data analyses, we compute, for different energy bins
E\in[E_{i}-\Delta E/2,E_{i}+\Delta E/2]
, the averaged electron antineutrino appearance probability,

\langle P_{i}\rangle=\frac{\mathop{\text{\LARGE$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-2.04861ptE_{i}-\Delta E/2$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle E_{i}+\Delta E/2$}}$}}\nolimits\phi(E)\sigma(E)P(E)dE}{\mathop{\text{\LARGE$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-2.04861ptE_{i}-\Delta E/2$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle E_{i}+\Delta E/2$}}$}}\nolimits\phi(E)\sigma(E)dE}\,,

(160)

and the expected flux of electron antineutrinos,

\langle\Phi_{i}\rangle=\frac{\mathop{\text{\LARGE$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-2.04861ptE_{i}-\Delta E/2$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle E_{i}+\Delta E/2$}}$}}\nolimits\phi(E)\sigma(E)P(E)dE}{\mathop{\text{\LARGE$\int_{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle\kern-2.04861ptE_{i}-\Delta E/2$}}^{\text{\normalsize$\scriptstyle E_{i}+\Delta E/2$}}$}}\nolimits\sigma(E)dE}\,.

(161)

We assume that the detection efficiency
\epsilon
is energy independent within each bin so that it cancels out when computing the ratios — see Equation
160
and Equation
161
.
We also considered perfect detector energy resolution — or at least an energy-independent one. We have checked that, for the KamLAND experiment, when including a realistic energy resolution of 6.4%/
\sqrt{E\text{(MeV)}}
, our results deviate in less than 0.5%. This is a consequence of
\bar{\nu}_{e}
appearance probability being a rather smooth function of neutrino energy — see the right panel of
Figure

23
.

The most restrictive limits so far have been derived using inverse beta decay as a detection channel. Hence, we focus on that process and restrict ourselves to energies above its threshold — which is of
\sim
1.8 MeV — where the only relevant contribution is due to
8
B neutrinos.
We compute the
\bar{\nu}_{e}
appearance probability and the expected flux numerically, using both the numerical and analytical expressions for the probabilities
P(\nu_{e}\to\bar{\nu}_{e})
.
In
Table

6
and
Table

7
we present these probabilities and the expected
\bar{\nu}_{e}
fluxes for
\mu_{12}=10^{-12}\mu_{B}
and
B_{\perp}(r_{0})=1
kG. As we have shown, to a very good extent, the electron antineutrino appearance probability — and therefore the
\bar{\nu}_{e}
flux — is proportional to
(\mu_{12}B_{\perp}(r_{0}))^{2}
. Then, the values of
\langle P_{i}\rangle
and
\langle\Phi_{i}\rangle
for different choices of
\mu_{12}B_{\perp}(r_{0})
can be found simply by rescaling.
